Let me think about the proof strategy.

This is a noninterference proof for STLC with security levels (a "SLam"-like calculus, actually this looks like the "Security" benchmark from a suite of noninterference proofs, e.g. from the "POPLmark"-ish benchmarks / the "Noninterference" benchmark). The structure is fairly standard: logical relation indexed by a security level/relation.

Let me deeply understand the language:

Types:
- Ty_Unit kappa
- Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa
- Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa

Security annotation: reader and indirect_reader. wf_security: flows_to indirect_reader reader (so indirect_reader ≤ reader in flow? Actually flows_to l1 l2 means l1 can flow to l2; High -> Low is False. So wf_security kappa says indirect_reader flows to reader, i.e., indirect reader is at most as secret as reader... hmm, actually if indirect_reader = High and reader = Low then High flows to Low = False, so this is disallowed. So indirect_reader <= reader).

Terms: unit with security; abs T body kappa; app t1 t2 r; pair t1 t2 kappa; fst t r; snd t r; inl/inr with kappa; case t0 body1 body2 r; protect l t; if c yes no r.

Typing:
- T_App: t1 : Arrow T1 T2 kappa; t2 : T1; flows_to kappa.reader r; result type ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T2.
- T_Fst: t : Prod T1 T2 kappa; flows_to kappa.reader r; result ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T1.
- T_Case: t : Sum T1 T2 kappa; flows_to kappa.reader r; branches; result ty_protect kappa.indirect_reader T.
- T_If: c : Sum (Unit public) (Unit public) k; yes/no : T; flows_to k.reader r; result ty_protect k.indirect_reader T.
- T_Protect: ty_protect l T.
- T_Sub.

Operational semantics:
- ST_AppAbs: app (abs T body kappa) v r --> protect kappa.indirect_reader (open body v), requires flows_to kappa.reader r.
- ST_FstPair similar: fst (pair v1 v2 kappa) r --> protect kappa.indirect_reader v1.
- ST_CaseLeft: case (inl v kappa) body1 body2 r --> protect kappa.indirect_reader (open body1 v).
- ST_ProtectValue: protect l v --> protect_value v l (for value v).
- ST_IfTrue: if (inl v k) yes no r --> protect k.indirect_reader yes.
- ST_IfFalse similarly.
- ST_Protect: protect l t --> protect l t' when t --> t'.

Erase_security erases all annotations.

Now the theorem: For closed t1, t2 of same secret type T_secret, placed in typed program context C with result type T_result ground and transparent and ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).indirect_reader (security_of T_result).indirect_reader, any evaluations of plug C t1 and plug C t2 produce equal erasures.

Hmm, so proof by logical relation. Because of the context C, we need a notion of "contextual equivalence" between t1 and t2, and then the theorem is that any two well-typed closed terms of the same type at a "protected" type are related... Actually, we need to be careful: The theorem wants: given t1, t2 both of type T_secret, and the context C such that plug C t1, plug C t2 : T_result, and T_result ground+transparent, and secret's indirect_reader cannot flow to result's indirect_reader, then results equal modulo erasure.

Standard approach in these security benchmarks: define a logical relation `interp` (or `equiv`) over types, parameterized by a "level" or a relation on labels. Something like:

`interp kappa T v1 v2` := the two values are observationally equivalent at type T w.r.t. observer level kappa.

The key security condition: if the type's security is "high" relative to the observer, then the relation is trivial (True), else the values must be structurally related.

Actually let me think about the standard formulation. These benchmarks (from the "Noninterference" suite, e.g., https://github.com/...? The benchmarks in "The Proof Challenge"?) Look: 
```
Fixpoint transparent_at (kappa : security) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  security_le (security_of T) kappa /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      transparent_at kappa T1 /\ transparent_at kappa T2
  end.
Definition transparent (T : ty) : Prop := transparent_at (security_of T) T.
```

### Understanding the calculus semantics and the intended noninterference

Types have security annotations `(reader, indirect_reader)` with wf_security: indirect_reader ↝ reader.

Typing judgment `has_type Gamma t T`.

Evaluation is deterministic and terminating (STLC with no recursion; all terms well-typed in a consistent context terminate; but the relation's fundamental theorem only needs that relatedness is proven for well-typed terms).

The theorem: two closed terms of the same type T_secret in a typed context C with ground+transparent result T_result, and secret.indirect_reader = High (because ~High↝Low), result.indirect_reader = Low (because... wait actually ~ flows_to secret.ir result.ir. flows_to is False only for (High, Low). Hmm, is ty_protect of wf_ty wf? wf_ty (Ty_Unit kappa) = wf_security kappa; ty_protect gives protect_security kappa l = (join reader l, join indirect l); wf_security requires join indirect l ↝ join reader l. #### Semantics intuition

A type T has an associated security (reader, indirect_reader). What does `reader` control? In the typing rules, `reader` is used as the *required* level r at elimination (app/fst/snd/case/if). E.g., app: if f : Arrow T1 T2 (κ) then to apply f you write `tm_app f a r` with flows_to κ.reader r. This is like: to use a function whose type is annotated with reader κ.reader, the program point label r must be at least κ.reader. And the result type is protected at κ.indirect_reader.

Note that `r` is just an annotation; semantically the result is independent of r. But the typing ensures that if κ.reader = High, you must use r = High, and then the result is protected at κ.indirect_reader... Hmm, `r` isn't propagated to the result; only κ.indirect_reader is.

The `indirect_reader` records: "who may learn that this value was computed in a context with...". protect_security joins both. ty_protect l T.

Now the theorem's observer is the result type's indirect_reader = Low. Why indirect_reader and not reader? Because the theorem is about the *result value* and the context C; the "indirect reader" of the result says who may know the result was produced within the context. Since the observation is just the erased result, maybe both matter.

Hmm, let's look at what the "transparent" condition gives: all subcomponents' security_le to the result's security: for each node U in T_result, reader(U) ↝ reader(T_result), indirect(U) ↝ indirect(T_result) = Low. So all nodes have indirect Low. Since wf_security probably holds for nodes appearing in typing derivations?... then reader ≥ indirect, but reader could still be High. Example: Ty_Unit (High, Low) — is that wf? wf_security: Low ↝ High = True. So yes, a node can have reader High, indirect Low. And transparent needs reader(U) ↝ reader(T_result), so reader(T_result) = High then.

What's the significance of reader Low vs High for a value? For values of type Unit, the annotation is erased anyway. Hmm, where does reader matter observably? In our relation, maybe the base relation at Unit should be: unobservable unless the reader is Low? But the relation needs to imply erased equality of the result value at T_result. If T_result = Ty_Unit (High, Low) (reader High, indirect Low), then the theorem says: any two closed terms of type T_secret plugged into a context producing this type have erased-equal results. But erased result of a unit value is always tm_unit public. So the unit base case is trivial.

The interesting base cases are sums and products: erased equality requires same constructor and recursively equal erasures.

Let's think about the relation that makes this true. At a sum type Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ with observer conditions, two values v1, v2 are related if both are inl with related payloads at T1, or both inr with related payloads at T2. Then erased equality follows from payload relations (recursively). At Unit: always true (any two units erase equal). At products: components related.

Now for the fundamental theorem and the secrecy condition, we need the "amnesia"/universality at high types. Consider a type T with indirect_reader High and observer level Low: the theorem's premise guarantees non-flow. But the relation for the *argument* type T_secret is used when the hole is plugged.

Hold on. Let's reconsider: the context lemma needs "if t1, t2 are related at T_secret at level ?, then plug C t1, plug C t2 related at T_result". Since t1, t2 are just two arbitrary closed terms of type T_secret, for this to hold we need the relation at T_secret (at the level used) to be *universal* — i.e., any two closed terms of type T_secret are related. That's the "high type = universal relation" idea, but here it's not because the observer level is low relative to the type's security... The level index: at type T_secret with indirect_reader High, observer Low: because ~flows_to High Low, relation is universal (True). Good. But wait, is that enough? The function that consumes the hole might do *anything* with it that's typable. The context lemma must show that any two terms satisfying the universal relation at T_secret can be plugged into C and still yield related results at T_result. Since the relation at T_secret is universal (all closed terms of type T_secret related), it says t1 and t2 are related, but the context lemma's correctness must be proven by induction on C, which requires the fundamental theorem and the arrow relation, etc.

So let's define the relation properly.

#### Designing the logical relation

I'll define a value relation `V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop` and term relation `E l T t1 t2 := ∀ v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2`. Hmm, but for values the relation needs to handle the "arrow" case where values are functions. Also we may want the relation to be defined on *arbitrary terms* for the environment.

The level `l` represents "the observer's level for indirect flows" (or perhaps a full security?). Let's think about what happens at arrow types with a level.

Suppose `l` is a label such that we only care about observations at l. Say l = Low (the result observer). For a type T with `flows_to (security_of T).indirect_reader l` false — i.e., indirect_reader = High and l = Low — the relation is universal.

For a type T with indirect_reader = Low (observable), what should the relation be? For Unit: maybe universal? Since all units erase to the same thing. For Sum/Prod: structural. For Arrow T1 T2 κ: what?

A function value at type Arrow T1 T2 κ contains code that might, when applied, use its argument. The argument type T1 may be high or low. The function's own indirect reader κ.indirect: since it's the type of the function value, and the observer l can see indirect flows through it iff κ.indirect ↝ l.

Standard logical relation for information flow (e.g., in "A Logical Relation for Noninterference" or the "sec" language) uses relations indexed by the *indirect reader level* of the *type*: at type T, the relation is universal if T's security is not visible to the observer, and otherwise structural. For arrows, relatedness means: for any two related arguments (at the same observer level? or at the argument type's level?), the results are related.

Hmm, but here's the thing: the function's argument could have High indirect_reader. If the function is allowed to branch on the argument (which it can if the argument is e.g. a sum type with high indirect_reader — wait, can it? To case on x : Sum T1 T2 κ with κ.indirect = High, we need flows_to κ.reader r and the case result is protected at κ.indirect = High, making the result High and unobservable. So branching on a high sum yields high results, which the function cannot return as low (protection only raises). Thus the function cannot leak the argument's high structure into a low result.

So the relation at an argument type T1 with High indirect_reader can be universal, but we must ensure that when the function's body cases on the argument, the case's result type is protected High and hence the relation there is universal, so any two branches (from different argument values) are related. That's the key mechanism.

So the relation at type U (for observer l) should be universal when indirect(U) doesn't flow to l, and structural otherwise.

Now, what about the arrow case recursion — at which level do we relate arguments and results? Let's think about the elimination rule: applying f : Arrow T1 T2 κ at label r gives result protected at κ.indirect. The rule requires flows_to κ.reader r. So if we relate two function values at observer l, and we feed them related arguments at T1 (at which level?), we need the applications to be related at ty_protect κ.indirect T2 at observer l.

Since ty_protect κ.indirect T2 has indirect_reader = join(κ.indirect, indirect(T2)) and reader = join(κ.reader, reader(T2)). If κ.indirect ↝ l then it's like relating at T2 with level l (plus extra reader). If not, universal.

Hmm, but here's a catch: when the function's own indirect_reader doesn't flow to l, the whole function relation is universal, so we don't care. If κ.indirect ↝ l (the function is visible to the observer), then the function was obtained by eliminating something... Hmm.

Wait, I realize the type's own security is what determines universality. But when applying a function, the result is protected by κ.indirect, not by T2's own security. That's consistent: the function is "visible" (κ.indirect Low) but its result type is secret, so the result values aren't observed. But then the function relation: what should it require?

Let me think about what the *function relation at observer l* should be when κ.indirect ↝ l:

Option A: ∀ w1 w2, V ? T1 w1 w2 -> E l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) (app v1 w1 r) (app v2 w2 r) for all r with flows_to κ.reader r.

But this would be too strong? Because the function might use its argument in a way that requires the argument relation to be at a different level. Hmm, but fundamentally, the function is just code; when we apply it to two related arguments, the applications should be related. If κ.indirect = Low but T2 is High, then result relation is universal — trivial. But consider: function f : Arrow T1 T2 κ with κ.indirect = Low and κ.reader = High, T2 low. The function was created by a lambda with body of type T2. When relating applications at observer Low, the argument relation at T1 uses level...? We need to know at what level to interpret T1. Hmm. If we use level l = Low for the argument, then T1 is interpreted with universality if T1.indirect = High. Is that right? The function body may case on x : T1 (if T1 is a sum) — the case requires flows_to T1.reader r and protects by T1.indirect. If T1.indirect = High, case result is High → unobservable. If T1.indirect = Low, the function can observe the structure of x directly, so the arguments must agree structurally for the applications to agree (at low result). But here's a subtlety: the function's type is Arrow T1 T2 κ, and the observer level for the *whole function* is l. When the function is applied, we relate arguments at level l. But hold on: what about the `reader` field? Where does it matter in the relation? Does r matter semantically? No, since r doesn't affect evaluation. #### Plan of the relation

Notation: `label` is the observer level.

Define `interp_l (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop` on terms? Let me define mutually:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  if flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l then
    match T with
    | Ty_Unit _ => True
    | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => (exists a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k? ...)
    ...
```

Hmm, matching sums: the value `tm_inl a k` contains its own annotation k, which may differ from the type's annotation due to subtyping/protection. So we should write: (∃ k a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k ∧ v2 = tm_inl a2 k) ∧ V l T1 a1 a2) ∨ (∃ k a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k ∧ v2 = tm_inr a2 k ∧ V l T2 a1 a2). Actually the annotations k on the two values may differ (they're erased anyway). Since the relation should eventually imply erased equality, and erase_security (tm_inl a k) = tm_inl (erase a) public, the k doesn't matter. So we can allow different k's.

Similarly products: ∃ k1 k2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = pair a1 b1 k1 ∧ v2 = pair a2 b2 k2 ∧ V l T1 a1 a2 ∧ V l T2 b1 b2.

Units: True (all unit values erase-equal). Hmm, but what about non-unit values? Since these are *values* related at a unit type. Could a value of type Unit be a pair? Not if well-typed, but our relation might be used on values whose form doesn't match. If we define Unit as True, then a pair and a unit would be related at Unit, which would break adequacy (erase pair ≠ erase unit) if a value of type Unit could be a pair. But well-typed values of unit type are units. We need a "canonical forms" lemma: has_type Gamma t T, value t, and T's head is Unit → t is tm_unit. But wait, subtyping: a value with type Unit could have been typed via T_Sub, e.g., t = tm_unit public : Ty_Unit public, subtype to Ty_Unit secret; both unit. Could t = tm_pair ... get type Ty_Unit? No subtype rule produces Unit from non-Unit. So canonical forms should hold structurally.

But for the relation, the cleaner approach: at Unit, require `∃ k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 ∧ v2 = tm_unit k2`. That's "both are units", which implies erased equality. Then canonical-forms lemmas are needed to know typed values are units. Adding these requirements makes adequacy easier (erased equality follows structurally) but requires more canonical form facts in the fundamental theorem. Alternatively, define Unit as True and prove adequacy by canonical forms at the top (ground type structure).

Hmm. Which is easier overall? In the fundamental theorem, for T_Unit, the term is tm_unit kappa, a value, and we need V l (Ty_Unit _) (tm_unit kappa) (tm_unit kappa) — True or ∃ks; trivial either way. For T_Sub and T_Var etc., the relation on values is only needed when the term is a value. For the variable case: env relation gives V l T (delta1 x) (delta2 x), values. For applications... the app case: term is tm_app t1 t2 r, not a value; relation is via evaluation: app t1 t2 r evaluates to v iff ... We need to reason about evaluations of applications, so we need the arrow relation to give the application behavior. The arrow relation will be defined as a "logical relation on function values" quantifying over related argument values and applications.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: in the fundamental theorem, when t is not a value, E l T t1 t2 is ∀ v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2. For the app case: t = tm_app t1 t2 r. From IH we get E at the arrow type for t1 and E at T1 for t2. Given evaluations `tm_app t1 t2 r ⇓ v1` and `⇓ v2`, we need to derive V l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) v1 v2. We should prove a small-step lemma: if t1 ⇓ abs-value w1 and t2 ⇓ val w2 then app t1 t2 r ⇓ v iff ... (protect κ.indirect (open body w2)) ⇓ v. Then use the arrow relation on the function values to get the applications related: `E l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) (tm_app w1 w2 r) (tm_app w1' w2' r)` where w1, w1' are function values; then V follows from evaluations.

To connect to the arrow relation's definition, the arrow relation on values should give: for related argument values a1,a2, `E l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) (tm_app v1 a1 r) (tm_app v2 a2 r)` for all r with flows_to κ.reader r. And then since app t1 t2 r ⇓ v1 iff app w1 w2 r ⇓ v1 (by determinism? or by multi-step composition), we can get V.

Actually, easier: prove a multi-step decomposition lemma: if t1 ⇓ w1 (value) and t2 ⇓ w2, then `multi (tm_app t1 t2 r) v ↔ multi (tm_app w1 w2 r) v`. Actually, one common approach: define the term relation as a *directed* relation `t1 ≤ t2` hmm no.

Alternatively, define `interp l T t1 t2` on terms directly with the relation being "step-compatible": e.g., define a relation that includes evaluation closure. Let me think about the cleanest formalization for the fundamental theorem:

Define `R l T t1 t2 := ∀ v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V l T v1 v2`. And `V` is defined as a Fixpoint on T with the arrow case referring to R at T1 and R at protected T2. Since R depends on V and V on R, we need mutual recursion. In Rocq, define V and R mutually:

```
Fixpoint interp (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => ...
  ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa => fun v1 v2 => ... uses R l T1 and R l (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2)
  end
with R (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop := fun t1 t2 => forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> interp l T v1 v2.
```

But wait: the arrow case needs `flows_to κ.reader r -> R ... (tm_app v1 a1 r) (tm_app v2 a2 r)`. That uses R at ty_protect κ.indirect T2. Mutually recursive definitions on T: V at Arrow refers to R at T1 and R at ty_protect κ.indirect T2. R at T1 refers to V at T1 — the structural recursion is fine (subterm), but R is a function that itself is defined mutually and takes T; the guard condition might be an issue: `with` block definitions where one function's body calls the other at a subterm. Rocq's guard checker should accept V's arrow case calling R (l T1) if T1 is a structural subterm of Arrow T1 T2 κ, and R's definition calls V at the same T (guard checker for mutual fixpoints allows calls between the two functions with the same or smaller argument? Actually the mutual fixpoint guard condition: recursive calls to any function in the block require an argument that is structurally smaller than the corresponding argument of the caller, where the "corresponding" is by position, and for mutual calls, the decreasing argument must be a subterm for the function being called... Hmm, I recall the guard condition for mutual fixpoints is subtle: in the body of f, calling g requires g's decreasing argument to be a strict subterm of f's decreasing argument. In R's body, calling V at the same T (not smaller) — since R's body is `fun t1 t2 => forall v1 v2, evaluates ... -> interp l T v1 v2`, calling interp at T (equal, not smaller) is not allowed by the guard checker I think (mutual recursion requires strictly decreasing? Hmm. That is, inline R as "∀ evaluations ... -> V". Wait, maybe we can define a relation `V l T v1 v2` with a *default/universal* case driven by security annotations, and for the arrow case, relate applications at `T2` but with a modified observer level?
```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => if flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l then True else True  (* always True *)
  ...
```

Let me define `S l T v1 v2` (structural relation) as a Fixpoint that:
- Ty_Unit: True

- Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ: both pairs with S l T1/S l T2 components
- Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ: ∀ r a1 a2, flows_to κ.reader r -> V l T1 a1 a2 -> (∀ u1 u2, evaluates (app v1 a1 r) u1 -> evaluates (app v2 a2 r) u2 -> V l (ty_protect κ.indirect T2) u1 u2)

Option 1: Define the relation by recursion on a *pair* (size of type, something) using well-founded recursion on the size of types. We could define `S` and `V` via `Fixpoint` on `size T + something`? Since ty_protect k T2 has the same size as T2, `size (ty_protect k T2) = size T2 < size (Arrow T1 T2 κ)`. So well-founded recursion on type size solves it! We can use `Program Fixpoint` or `Fixpoint` with an explicit fuel: define relations indexed by a natural number fuel equal to size of type, and prove that the recursion is on smaller size. So the relation `V l T κ v1 v2` where κ is the effective top-level security:
```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (κ : security) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  if flows_to κ.(indirect_reader) l then
    match T with
    | Ty_Unit _ => True
    | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ =>
        (∃ k1 k2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 ∧ V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2)
        ∨ (∃ k1 k2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 ∧ V l T2 (security_of T2) a1 a2)
    | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ =>
        ∃ k1 k2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 ∧
          V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2 ∧ V l T2 (security_of T2) b1 b2
    | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
        ∀ r a1 a2, flows_to κ.(reader) r -> V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2 ->
          ∀ u1 u2, evaluates (tm_app v1 a1 r) u1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 a2 r) u2 ->
            V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) κ.(indirect_reader)) u1 u2
    end
  else True.
```

Hmm, this is a well-formed Fixpoint on T. But hold on: for values at arrow types, `V` quantifies over all r with flows_to κ.reader r. But what if there's no such r? flows_to Low r is true for any r; flows_to High r only for r=High, so there's always at least one r. Good, non-vacuous.

Also, should the arrow relation quantify over *all* arguments a1, a2 (terms), requiring `V l T1 ... a1 a2` as a *value* relation (values) or term relation with evaluation? The applications are `tm_app v1 a1 r`; if a1 is a non-value, evaluation proceeds left-to-right: v1 is a value (since V on arrows is only applied to values in practice), then a1 steps. Relating at T1 via V as a value relation requires a1, a2 to be values. In the fundamental theorem app case, the argument t2 is related via the term relation, not necessarily a value; but at the point of application, the argument evaluates to a value a1 with V. Then the applications `tm_app v1 t2 r` evaluate by first reducing t2 to a1, then applying. Let me think about the semantic meaning. In these security calculi, the type of a function Arrow T1 T2 κ and the rule for application: given f : Arrow T1 T2 κ and a : T1, applying at label r with κ.reader ↝ r yields result protected at κ.indirect. The security of the function κ determines the "effects" of applying it. The argument type T1 has its own security for its own structure.

When are two functions related? Semantically (for noninterference), two function values f1, f2 are equivalent at observer l if for any two arguments a1, a2 that the observer cannot distinguish (at type T1 at level l), the applications are indistinguishable (at T2 at level l). The argument's own security determines what the observer can distinguish: if T1's indirect is High (unobservable to Low observer), then *all* arguments are indistinguishable, so the function must produce the same result for arbitrary arguments (that's the noninterference guarantee: no leaking of high inputs).

So argument relation uses level l and T1's own security: `V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2`. The subtype lemma for argument types is needed only when we compare the function values at the *same* observer level l. Since f's type is Arrow T1 T2 κ1 and U's is Arrow U1 U2 κ2 with U1 <: T1: when the observer passes an argument of type U1 (a "public" supertype), the function expecting T1 must accept it. Now, the fundamental theorem. Let's define the substitution/environment machinery.

Since terms are locally nameless with `open` and `subst`, and typing decomposes binders via `open body (tm_fvar x)` for fresh x, we can set up the standard "semantic typing" with environments mapping free variables (atoms) to terms (values).

But terms can also contain `tm_bvar`? As discussed, well-typed terms are locally closed. But our fundamental theorem is about has_type Gamma t T for arbitrary t; we should prove that well-typed terms are locally closed, or incorporate `lc_at` into the environment/typing.

`forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall l δ1 δ2, env_rel l Gamma δ1 δ2 -> R l T Low (subst_all δ1 t) (subst_all δ2 t)`

where `subst_all δ t` substitutes each free var x by δ x, keeping bvars. Hmm, but t might contain bvars (if not locally closed). However, has_type Gamma t T probably implies t is locally closed — let's just prove that (needed also for `value` facts).

  So define:

  ```
  Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
    match (security_of T).(indirect_reader) with
    | High => True
    | Low =>
      match T with
      | Ty_Unit _ => True
      | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ => (∃ k1 k2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl a2 k2 ∧ V T1 a1 a2)
                          ∨ (∃ k1 k2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inr a2 k2 ∧ V T2 a1 a2)
      | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => ∃ k1 k2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 ∧ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 ∧ V T1 a1 a2 ∧ V T2 b1 b2
      | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => ∀ r a1 a2, flows_to κ.(reader) r -> R T1 a1 a2 -> R T2 (tm_app v1 a1 r) (tm_app v2 a2 r)
      end
    end
  ```
  where R T t1 t2 := ∀ v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> V T v1 v2.

  ```
        (∀ u1 u2, evaluates (tm_app v1 a1 r) u1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 a2 r) u2 -> V T2 u1 u2)
  ```

    Hmm, wait. Let's step back and think about whether the relation should be defined on *values* where we use the value's annotations rather than the type's. - Goal structural: join (indirect T1) κ.ind = Low → κ.ind = Low, indirect T1 = Low.

Let me now redefine V:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to_b κ.indirect_reader Low then
        (∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl a2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T1 a1 a2)
        ∨ (∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_inr a2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T2 a1 a2)
      else True
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to_b κ.ind Low then
        ∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T1 a1 a2 ∧ V T2 b1 b2
      else True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      if flows_to_b κ.ind Low then
        (∃ T0 B κ1 T0' B' κ1', v1 = tm_abs T0 B κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_abs T0' B' κ1' ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ1' κ ∧
          ∀ r a1 a2, flows_to κ.reader r -> R T1 a1 a2 -> R T2 (app v1 a1 r) (app v2 a2 r))
      else True
  end
```

Solution: use R in the env relation instead of V. Then the fundamental theorem's T_Var case works directly (goal R T (δ x) (δ x) = env hypothesis) ✓. And the env relation would be `R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x)` plus value-ness? So env_rel can be: (∀x, value (δ1 x) ∧ value (δ2 x)) ∧ (∀ x T, lookup x Gamma = Some T → R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x)). Hmm, but then for the initial environment mapping x ↦ t1, t2, we need value t1? The value-ness is needed for... the substitution lemma: lc_at preservation requires δ values to be locally closed. Values are locally closed ✓ but t1, t2 are not values. Hmm. So we need δ x to be locally closed for the lc preservation, and value for the R→V conversions? Let's see where value-ness of δ x is used:
- lc preservation under subst_all: need each δ y locally closed (lc_at 0). Closed well-typed terms are locally closed ✓ (we prove has_type empty t T -> locally_closed t, and fv t = [] for empty context? need fv as well for closedness but lc is enough).
- In T_Var: R from env — no value needed.
- Extending the environment in T_Abs with w1 from an evaluation: w1 is a value ✓, and R T1 w1 w2 from V T1 w1 w2 via multi_value ✓ (value).

So define env_rel as:
```
env_rel Gamma δ1 δ2 :=
  (forall x, locally_closed (δ1 x) /\ locally_closed (δ2 x) /\ value? )
  /\ (forall x T, lookup x Gamma = Some T -> R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x))
```
Hmm, wait: do we need value-ness at all? In the T_Abs case we build δ' = δ[x↦w] with w a value. The env relation's lookup condition for x: V T1 w1 w2 → R T1 w1 w2 (needs values ✓). For other y: R T (δ y) from the old env ✓. And lc: w is a value → lc ✓; δ y lc from env.

So:
```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x, locally_closed (δ1 x) /\ locally_closed (δ2 x)) /\
  (forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x)).
```
Hmm, but actually for the environment used in the context lemma (δ = [x ↦ t1]), we need locally_closed t1 — from has_type empty t1 T_secret ✓ (we prove that). And R T_secret t1 t2 — this is what we need to establish from the theorem's hypotheses: t1, t2 : T_secret with T_secret.ind = High → V T_secret = True... but R T_secret t1 t2 := ∀ evaluations, V T_secret. Since V T_secret = True when T_secret's indirect = High: is that right for all type constructors? V Unit = True ✓; V Sum with κ.ind=High → True ✓; V Prod ✓; V Arrow with κ.ind=High → True ✓.
```
Definition security_le_b?  -- security_le is Prop.

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => ∃ κ1 κ2 : security, v1 = tm_unit κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_unit κ2
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl a2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T1 a1 a2)
          ∨ (∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_inr a2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          ∃ κ1 κ2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 κ2 ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ2 κ ∧ V T1 a1 a2 ∧ V T2 b1 b2
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          ∃ T0 B κ1 T0' B' κ1', v1 = tm_abs T0 B κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_abs T0' B' κ1' ∧ security_le κ1 κ ∧ security_le κ1' κ ∧
          ∀ r a1 a2, flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
             (∀ w1 w2, evaluates a1 w1 -> evaluates a2 w2 -> V T1 w1 w2) ->
             (∀ u1 u2, evaluates (tm_app v1 a1 r) u1 -> evaluates (tm_app v2 a2 r) u2 -> V T2 u1 u2)
      end
  end.
```

```
Definition ind (κ : security) : label := κ.(indirect_reader).

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      match ind κ with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists κ1 κ2, v1 = tm_unit κ1 /\ v2 = tm_unit κ2
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      match ind κ with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 κ2 /\

              security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>

      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists κ1 κ2 a1 a2 b1 b2,
            v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 κ2 /\
            security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2

      end
  end.
```

```coq
Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l, flows_to l l.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall a b c, flows_to a b -> flows_to b c -> flows_to a c.
Proof. destruct a, b, c; simpl; auto. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_join_l : forall a b, flows_to a (join a b).
```

Let me write this in a robust style: 
```
Lemma evaluates_app : forall t1 t2 r u,
  evaluates (tm_app t1 t2 r) u ->
  exists T0 B κ0 a,
    multi t1 (tm_abs T0 B κ0) /\ value (tm_abs T0 B κ0) /\
    evaluates t2 a /\ flows_to κ0.(reader) r /\
    multi (tm_protect κ0.(indirect_reader) (open B a)) u.
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r u [Hm Hv].
  remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as X eqn:E.
  revert t1 t2 r E.
  induction Hm as [X | X y z hs hrest IH]; intros t1 t2 r E; subst.
  - inversion Hv.
  - inversion hs as [... | ...]; subst; clear hs.
```

```coq
Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l, flows_to l l.

  flows_to a b -> flows_to b c -> flows_to a c.
Proof. destruct a, b, c; simpl; auto. Qed.

Proof. destruct a, b; simpl; auto. Qed.

Proof. destruct a, b; simpl; auto. Qed.

  flows_to (join a b) c <-> flows_to a c /\ flows_to b c.
Proof. destruct a, b, c; simpl; tauto. Qed.

Lemma join_high_r : forall a, join a High = High.
Proof. destruct a; reflexivity. Qed.

  security_le a b -> security_le b c -> security_le a c.
Proof.
  intros [ra ia] [rb ib] [rc ic]; simpl.
  intros [h1 h2] [h3 h4]; split; eapply flows_to_trans; eauto.
```

Let me write:

```coq
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => exists κ1 κ2 : security, v1 = tm_unit κ1 /\ v2 = tm_unit κ2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          (exists (κ1 κ2 : security) (a1 a2 : tm),
              v1 = tm_inl a1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 κ2 /\
              security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T1 a1 a2)
          \/ (exists (κ1 κ2 : security) (a1 a2 : tm),
              v1 = tm_inr a1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 κ2 /\
              security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T2 a1 a2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          exists (κ1 κ2 : security) (a1 a2 b1 b2 : tm),
            v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 κ2 /\
            security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      match κ.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          exists (T0 : ty) (B : tm) (κ1 : security) (T0' : ty) (B' : tm) (κ1' : security),
            v1 = tm_abs T0 B κ1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T0' B' κ1' /\
            security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ1' κ /\
            (forall (r : label) (a1 a2 : tm),
                flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
                (forall w1 w2, evaluates a1 w1 -> evaluates a2 w2 -> V T1 w1 w2) ->
                (forall u1 u2, evaluates (tm_app v1 a1 r) u1 ->
                               evaluates (tm_app v2 a2 r) u2 -> V T2 u1 u2))
      | High => True
      end
  end.
```
This should be accepted if the guard checker sees T1/T2 as subterms. The match on `κ.(indirect_reader)` is inside; recursive calls in branches of both matches. I believe Rocq accepts since the recursive argument T is the outer match scrutinee and T1/T2 are its subterms.

Hmm, one risk: the guard checker may complain because the recursive calls are under the inner match on κ.ind, whose scrutinee is not a subterm of T... but that shouldn't matter; guard checking only cares that recursive calls are on structural subterms of the recursive argument. Nested matches on other terms are fine.

Let me write evaluates_app following the same pattern.

```coq
Lemma evaluates_app : forall (t1 t2 : tm) (r : label) (u : tm),
    evaluates (tm_app t1 t2 r) u ->
    exists (T0 : ty) (B : tm) (κ0 : security) (a : tm),
      multi t1 (tm_abs T0 B κ0) /\ value (tm_abs T0 B κ0) /\
      evaluates t2 a /\ flows_to κ0.(reader) r /\
      multi (tm_protect κ0.(indirect_reader) (open B a)) u.
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r u [Hm Hv].
  remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as X eqn:E.
  revert t1 t2 r Hv E.
  induction Hm as [X | X y z hs hrest IH]; intros t1 t2 r Hv E.
  - rewrite E in Hv; inversion Hv.
  - rewrite E in hs; inversion hs; subst; clear hs.
```
Inversion cases:
1. ST_AppAbs T B κ v r0 (Hval : value (tm_abs T B κ)) (Hvalv : value v) (Hflow : flows_to κ.reader r0): the equation from inversion gives t1 = tm_abs T B κ, t2 = v, r = r0, y = tm_protect κ.ind (open B v). After subst, the goal: exists T0 B0 κ0 a, multi (tm_abs T B κ) (tm_abs T0 B0 κ0) /\ value ... /\ evaluates v a /\ flows_to κ0.reader r0 /\ multi (tm_protect κ0.ind (open B0 a)) z.
   Take T0=T, B0=B, κ0=κ, a=v:
   - multi (tm_abs T B κ) (tm_abs T B κ) ✓ refl.
   - value ✓ Hval.
   - evaluates v v ✓ (refl + Hvalv).

**evaluates_fst**:
```
Lemma evaluates_fst : forall (t : tm) (r : label) (u : tm),
  evaluates (tm_fst t r) u ->
  exists (v1 v2 : tm) (κ0 : security),
    multi t (tm_pair v1 v2 κ0) /\ value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    flows_to κ0.(reader) r /\
    multi (tm_protect κ0.(indirect_reader) v1) u.
```
Step rules with fst LHS: ST_FstPair (v1 v2 κ r, value v1, value v2, flows) → t = pair, y = protect κ.ind v1; ST_Fst (t --> t') → y = fst t'.

Proof pattern analogous.

**evaluates_snd**: analogous with ST_SndPair, ST_Snd.

**evaluates_case**:
```
Lemma evaluates_case : forall (t b1 b2 : tm) (r : label) (u : tm),
  evaluates (tm_case t b1 b2 r) u ->
  (exists (v : tm) (κ0 : security),
     multi t (tm_inl v κ0) /\ value v /\ flows_to κ0.reader r /\
     multi (tm_protect κ0.ind (open b1 v)) u)
  \/ (exists (v : tm) (κ0 : security),
     multi t (tm_inr v κ0) /\ value v /\ flows_to κ0.reader r /\
     multi (tm_protect κ0.ind (open b2 v)) u).
```
Step rules: ST_CaseLeft (v κ b1 b2 r, value v, flows) → y = protect κ.ind (open b1 v); ST_CaseRight; ST_Case (t --> t').

**evaluates_if**:
```
Lemma evaluates_if : forall (c yes no : tm) (r : label) (u : tm),
  evaluates (tm_if c yes no r) u ->
  (exists (v : tm) (κ0 : security),
     multi c (tm_inl v κ0) /\ value v /\ flows_to κ0.reader r /\
     multi (tm_protect κ0.ind yes) u)
  \/ (exists (v : tm) (κ0 : security),
     multi c (tm_inr v κ0) /\ value v /\ flows_to κ0.reader r /\
     multi (tm_protect κ0.ind no) u).
```
Step rules: ST_If (c --> c'), ST_IfTrue, ST_IfFalse.

Now the big remaining piece: the fundamental theorem. Let me plan it in detail and write it.

```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall (x : atom) (T : ty), lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x).
```

Fundamental theorem:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm), env_rel Gamma δ1 δ2 ->
  R T (subst_all δ1 t) (subst_all δ2 t).
```

Wait, I need to define `subst_all`:
```
Fixpoint subst_all (δ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => δ x
  | tm_unit κ => tm_unit κ
  | tm_abs T B κ => tm_abs T (subst_all δ B) κ
  | tm_app a b r => tm_app (subst_all δ a) (subst_all δ b) r
  ...
  end.
```

And the identity: `subst_all δ (open_rec k u body) = open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body)` — hmm, is this exactly right? Let me think again with the definition of open_rec:

open_rec k u t:
- tm_bvar i => if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i

So open_rec k u body replaces bvar k with u.

Claim: subst_all δ (open_rec k u body) = open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body).

Proof by induction on body generalizing k:
- body = tm_bvar i: LHS: subst_all δ (if i =? k then u else bvar i) = if i =? k then subst_all δ u else bvar i. RHS: open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ (bvar i)) = if i =? k then subst_all δ u else bvar i ✓.
- body = tm_fvar x: LHS: subst_all δ (fvar x) = δ x. RHS: open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (δ x) — hmm! This recursively applies open_rec to δ x, which is wrong if δ x contains bvar k. The RHS would replace bvars in δ x. So the identity FAILS in general when δ x contains bvars.

In our use, δ maps to values (or at least locally closed terms), so δ x has no bvars, and then open_rec does nothing to it. But the identity as stated is false in general. Fix: require that δ x is "closed" (lc_at anything?). If δ x has no bvars (lc_at 0), then open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (δ x) = δ x = open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (δ x)? Hmm, open_rec k v (δ x) = δ x since δ x has no bvars ✓ (open_rec only changes bvars).

Wait, but also the terms introduced by open_rec (u) may contain bvars that get captured; both sides do the same ✓.

Also, if δ x contains bvars, subst_all doesn't substitute them, but the RHS's open_rec would modify them — that's the failure. So assume: for all x, lc_at 0 (δ x) (locally closed at 0, no bvars).

Alternatively, formulate the identity as `subst_all δ (open_rec k u body) = open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body)` under the hypothesis `forall x, lc_at 0 (δ x)` (and no condition on u? u appears as subst_all δ u on the RHS; on the left, u is inserted verbatim then subst_all applies to it and under binders... hmm wait, let me redo:

LHS: subst_all δ (open_rec k u body). open_rec inserts u verbatim; then subst_all recurses into it (substituting fvars in u), including under binders — but that's the same as subst_all δ u ✓ as long as recursing under binders doesn't matter for fvars (fvars aren't affected by binders) ✓.

So the problematic case is only when subst_all encounters a term inserted by open_rec under a binder and open_rec on the RHS would capture its bvars. Specifically, the RHS's open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body) inserts subst_all δ u at positions where bvar k occurred, but under binders it looks for bvar (k+j) — while the LHS's open_rec inserted u at those positions and then subst_all recursed under the binder into u's bvars? No—subst_all doesn't change bvars. So at a position under j binders, LHS has u (with its bvars unchanged), RHS has subst_all δ u (fvars substituted) — but also, if δ x's bvars exist...

```
Lemma subst_all_open_rec : forall δ, (forall x, lc_at 0 (δ x)) ->
```

Proof by induction on body generalizing k.

The replacement index must be ≥ n for nothing to be replaced: if all bvars < n and k ≥ n then no bvar equals k ✓. So the general lemma: `lc_at n t -> forall k, n <= k -> open_rec k u t = t`. Provable by induction on t generalizing n, k.
  In the abs case: lc_at n (abs T body κ) = lc_at (S n) body; need open_rec (S k) u body = body with S n ≤ S k ✓ from n ≤ k ✓ and IH with n' = S n, k' = S k ✓.

Careful: `lc_at n` counts free bvars < n; substituting at index k with k ≥ n means no free bvar equals k ✓. Under a binder, open_rec uses k+1 and lc_at uses n+1, preserving n ≤ k ✓. Good.

- unit: ✓.
- abs T body κ: LHS = subst_all δ (abs T (open_rec (S k) u body) κ) = abs T (subst_all δ (open_rec (S k) u body)) κ = abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body)) κ (by IH with k := S k) = RHS ✓.

Proving `has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t` seems doable with the open/lc lemma we discussed. Let me prove it:
```
Lemma has_type_lc : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t.
```
By induction on the typing derivation:
- T_Var: lc_at 0 (fvar x) = True ✓.
- T_Unit ✓.
- T_Abs: need lc_at 1 body. We have for all x ∉ L, has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2, so by IH lc_at 0 (open body (fvar x)). Need lc_at 1 body from that. Let me re-derive: the lemma `lc_at (S k) body -> lc_at k (open_rec k u body)` (opening preserves lc) and a converse. The converse we need: lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body) -> lc_at 1 body.

  Then in T_Abs: lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body) -> lc_at 1 body ✓.
- T_Case: need lc_at 1 body1, body2 from the opened typings ✓ same lemma.
- T_App: lc_at 0 (app t1 t2 r) = lc t1 ∧ lc t2 ✓ from IHs.
- T_Pair, T_Inl, T_Inr, T_Fst, T_Snd, T_Protect, T_Sub, T_If: straightforward.

So has_type_lc is provable with:
- `lc_open_inv : forall k u body, lc_at k (open_rec k u body) -> lc_at (S k) body` (as derived).
- monotonicity: lc_at n t -> n ≤ m -> lc_at m t? Hmm, do we need it? For T_Abs from lc_at 1 body we conclude lc_at 0 (abs ...) = lc_at 1 body ✓ directly.

Hmm, but wait: do we also need `fv`? For the substitution identity `subst_all δ' (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst_all δ body) w` we needed x ∉ fv body. Yes, we need fv too. Let me now define the identity lemmas needed:

1. `lc_at_open_rec_inv : forall k u body, lc_at k (open_rec k u body) -> lc_at (S k) body.`
2. `lc_at_open_rec_id : forall t n, lc_at n t -> forall k u, n <= k -> open_rec k u t = t.`
3. `subst_all_open_rec : forall δ, (forall x, lc_at 0 (δ x)) -> forall k u body, subst_all δ (open_rec k u body) = open_rec k (subst_all δ u) (subst_all δ body).`
4. `subst_all_ext : forall δ1 δ2 t, (forall x, In x (fv t) -> δ1 x = δ2 x) -> subst_all δ1 t = subst_all δ2 t.`
5. `has_type_lc : has_type Gamma t T -> lc_at 0 t.`
6. `open_subst_all : forall δ x w body, lc_at 0 w -> (forall y, lc_at 0 (δ y)) -> x ∉ fv body -> subst_all (extend δ x w) (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body) = open_rec 0 w (subst_all δ body).`
   where extend δ x w := fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w else δ y.
   From (3): subst_all δ' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body) = open_rec 0 (subst_all δ' (fvar x)) (subst_all δ' body) = open_rec 0 w (subst_all δ' body). And subst_all δ' body = subst_all δ body if δ' = δ on fv body; since x ∉ fv body, for y ∈ fv body, y ≠ x → δ' y = δ y ✓ (using Nat.eqb_neq).
   Note (3) requires δ' to map to lc_at 0 terms: δ' y = w (lc ✓) or δ y (lc ✓).

First, the lc/subst_all infrastructure. Let me write:

```coq
(* ===== locally-closed and substitution infrastructure ===== *)
```

Simpler: prove `exists x, forall y, In y L -> y < x` by induction:
```
Lemma fresh_atom : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L [x Hx]].
  - exists 0; simpl; auto.
  - exists (S (Nat.max a x)). intros [H | H].
    + apply Nat.lt_irrefl in ... (* a = S (max a x) impossible *)
    + apply Hx in H; pose (Nat.le_max_r a x); lia.
Qed.
```
For the first: a < S (max a x) since a ≤ max a x < S(...) ✓ lia. H : a = S (max a x) → lia.
For the second: H : In x0 L; Hx : ~ In x0 L → contradiction ✓.
So:
```
  - exists (S (Nat.max a x)). intros [Ha | Hin].
    + lia.
    + exact (Hx Hin).
```
Note: the exists x variable shadows; let me rename.

`exact (Hx Hin)` : Hx : ~ In x0 L = (In x0 L -> False) applied to Hin : In x0 L ✓.

Also `fv_ctx` and the plug lemma.

Let me think about `fv_ctx`:
```
Fixpoint fv_ctx (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' κ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t r => fv_ctx C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' r => fv t ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t κ => fv_ctx C' ++ fv t
  | C_PairRight t C' κ => fv t ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_Fst C' r => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Snd C' r => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Inl C' κ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Inr C' κ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' b1 b2 r => fv_ctx C' ++ fv b1 ++ fv b2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' b2 r => fv t0 ++ fv_ctx C' ++ fv b2
  | C_CaseRight t0 b1 C' r => fv t0 ++ fv b1 ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_Protect l C' => fv_ctx C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n r => fv_ctx C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n r => fv c ++ fv_ctx C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' r => fv c ++ fv y ++ fv_ctx C'
  end.
```

Then:
```
Lemma subst_all_id_on_fv : forall δ t, (forall x, In x (fv t) -> δ x = tm_fvar x) -> subst_all δ t = t.
Lemma subst_all_plug : forall δ C t, (forall x, In x (fv_ctx C) -> δ x = tm_fvar x) ->
   subst_all δ (plug C t) = plug C (subst_all δ t).
```
Proof by induction on C; each case uses subst_all_id_on_fv for the other subterms and IH for the plugged subterm.

Let me write fv_ctx and subst_all_plug.

```
Fixpoint fv_ctx (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t _ => fv_ctx C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t _ => fv_ctx C' ++ fv t
  | C_PairRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_Fst C' _ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Snd C' _ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Inl C' _ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_Inr C' _ => fv_ctx C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' t1 t2 _ => fv_ctx C' ++ fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' t2 _ => fv t0 ++ fv_ctx C' ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseRight t0 t1 C' _ => fv t0 ++ fv t1 ++ fv_ctx C'
  | C_Protect _ C' => fv_ctx C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n _ => fv_ctx C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n _ => fv c ++ fv_ctx C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' _ => fv c ++ fv y ++ fv_ctx C'
  end.

Lemma subst_all_plug : forall (δ : atom -> tm) (C : program_context) (t : tm),
  (forall x, In x (fv_ctx C) -> δ x = tm_fvar x) ->
  subst_all δ (plug C t) = plug C (subst_all δ t).
Proof.
  intros δ C; induction C as [...] ; intros t H; simpl.
```
Each case: plug C t = e.g. tm_app (plug C' t) t2 r. subst_all of that = tm_app (subst_all δ (plug C' t)) (subst_all δ t2) r. By IH (with the premise for C' — derived from H for fv_ctx C' ⊆ fv_ctx C) and subst_all_id_on_fv for t2 (from H's condition on fv t2).

For each case I need:
- IH: forall t, (forall x, In x (fv_ctx C') -> δ x = tm_fvar x) -> subst_all δ (plug C' t) = plug C' (subst_all δ t).
- The premise for C': In x (fv_ctx C') -> In x (fv_ctx C) → H ✓ with the appropriate In_app lemmas.
- subst_all_id_on_fv for the other terms with In x (fv t2) -> In x (fv_ctx C).

Let me write the lemma with the C induction. The `as` pattern for C's constructors: 
1. C_Hole: []

1. C_Hole: `simpl; reflexivity.`
2. C_Abs T C' κ: `simpl. rewrite IHC. reflexivity. { intros x Hx; apply H; exact Hx. }` Hmm, after `rewrite IHC` the premise subgoal is generated; then `reflexivity` applies to the main goal only if it's the first. Let me write:
   `simpl. rewrite IHC. reflexivity. intros x Hx; apply H; exact Hx.` — but the bullet structure: after `-`, the tactics apply to the goal; `rewrite IHC` creates 2 subgoals (main + premise); `reflexivity` closes the main; then the remaining premise is handled by the next tactic in the same bullet? 3. C_AppLeft C' t2 r: `simpl. rewrite IHC. rewrite subst_all_id_on_fv. reflexivity. intros x Hx; apply H; apply In_app_l; exact Hx. intros x Hx; apply H; apply In_app_r; exact Hx.`

Hmm, the order may be: after rewrite IHC: [main, premIHC]; rewrite id on main → [main, premId]; reflexivity → [premIHC, premId]? Or [premId, premIHC]? Coq's goal ordering: rewrite generates the side conditions *after* the main goal. So after rewrite IHC: goal1 = main, goal2 = premIHC. Then `rewrite subst_all_id_on_fv` applies to goal1, generating goal1' = main', goal2' = premId, goal3 = premIHC. reflexivity closes goal1'. Remaining: goal2' (premId) then goal3 (premIHC). Hmm, or the side conditions get appended in order... Let me not rely on the order and instead use `; auto` with explicit premise proofs via assert.

Cleaner: avoid conditional rewrites by proving the premises as local facts first, then using `rewrite (IHC Hprem)` with the premise explicitly applied. Since IHC is a local hypothesis (forall t, premise -> eq), `rewrite (IHC t Hprem)` works if I have Hprem. Let me do:

Let me define these to make the fundamental theorem cleaner:

```
Lemma V_unit_inv : forall (κ : security) (v1 v2 : tm),
    κ.(indirect_reader) = Low -> V (Ty_Unit κ) v1 v2 ->
    exists κ1 κ2, v1 = tm_unit κ1 /\ v2 = tm_unit κ2.
Proof.
  intros κ v1 v2 H HV; destruct κ as [r i]; destruct i; [| discriminate H].
  simpl in HV |- *. exact HV.
Qed.

Lemma V_sum_inv : forall (T1 T2 : ty) (κ : security) (v1 v2 : tm),
    κ.(indirect_reader) = Low -> V (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 ->
    ((exists κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inl a1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 κ2 /\
        security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T1 a1 a2)
     \/ (exists κ1 κ2 a1 a2, v1 = tm_inr a1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 κ2 /\
        security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T2 a1 a2)).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 κ v1 v2 H HV; destruct κ as [r i]; destruct i; [| discriminate H].
  simpl in HV |- *. exact HV.
Qed.

Lemma V_prod_inv : forall (T1 T2 : ty) (κ : security) (v1 v2 : tm),
    κ.(indirect_reader) = Low -> V (Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 ->
    exists κ1 κ2 a1 a2 b1 b2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 κ1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 κ2 /\
      security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ2 κ /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2.
Proof. ... Qed.

Lemma V_arrow_inv : forall (T1 T2 : ty) (κ : security) (v1 v2 : tm),
    κ.(indirect_reader) = Low -> V (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ) v1 v2 ->
    exists T0 B κ1 T0' B' κ1', v1 = tm_abs T0 B κ1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T0' B' κ1' /\
      security_le κ1 κ /\ security_le κ1' κ /\
      (forall r a1 a2, flows_to κ.(reader) r ->
         (forall w1 w2, evaluates a1 w1 -> evaluates a2 w2 -> V T1 w1 w2) ->
         (forall u1 u2, evaluates (tm_app v1 a1 r) u1 ->
                        evaluates (tm_app v2 a2 r) u2 -> V T2 u1 u2)).
Proof. ... Qed.
```

These are all proven by destructing the record, destructing the indirect label, discriminating, simpl, exact HV.

Let me now write the fundamental theorem. I'll write it in one go, then compile and fix errors. Given its size, I'll write carefully.

```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x : atom, lc_at 0 (δ1 x) /\ lc_at 0 (δ2 x)) /\
  (forall (x : atom) (T : ty), lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R T (δ1 x) (δ2 x)).

Lemma R_of_V_value : forall (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm),
    value v1 -> value v2 -> V T v1 v2 -> R T v1 v2.
Proof.
  intros T v1 v2 Hv1 Hv2 HV u1 u2 [Hm1 _] [Hm2 _].
  rewrite (multi_value v1 u1 Hv1 Hm1). rewrite (multi_value v2 u2 Hv2 Hm2).
  exact HV.
Qed.
```
Hmm, multi_value : forall v u, value v -> multi v u -> u = v. So `multi_value v1 u1 Hv1 Hm1 : u1 = v1`. Rewriting u1 to v1 in the goal V T u1 u2 ✓. Then u2 → v2 ✓.

Now the fundamental theorem:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
    has_type Gamma t T ->
    forall (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm), env_rel Gamma δ1 δ2 ->
    R T (subst_all δ1 t) (subst_all δ2 t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [...same pattern as has_type_lc...];
    intros δ1 δ2 [Hlc HenvR].
```

Lemma security_le_ind_low : forall (κ1 κ2 : security),
    security_le κ1 κ2 -> κ2.(indirect_reader) = Low -> κ1.(indirect_reader) = Low.

Lemma env_rel_extend : forall (Gamma : context) (δ1 δ2 : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (T1 : ty) (w1 w2 : tm),
    env_rel Gamma δ1 δ2 -> value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 ->

      unfold update in Hlook. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in Hlook. injection Hlook as HT'. subst T'.

      rewrite E. apply HenvR; exact Hlook.
Qed.

All the auxiliary lemmas compile. Now the final theorem. Let me write it.

```
Theorem noninterference : forall (C : program_context) (t1 t2 : tm)
    (T_secret T_result : ty),
  has_type empty t1 T_secret ->
  has_type empty t2 T_secret ->
  context_has_type C T_secret T_result ->
  ground T_result ->
  transparent T_result ->
  ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)
      (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) ->
  same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).
Proof.
  intros C t1 t2 T_secret T_result Ht1 Ht2 HC Hg Htr Hni.
  destruct (nflow_to_low _ _ Hni) as [Hsi Hri].
  destruct HC as [L HL].
  destruct (fresh_atom (L ++ fv_ctx C)) as [x Hx].
  assert (HxL : ~ In x L) by (intro Hc; apply Hx; apply In_app_l; exact Hc).
  assert (HxC : ~ In x (fv_ctx C)) by (intro Hc; apply Hx; apply In_app_r; exact Hc).
  assert (Hty : has_type (update empty x T_secret) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) T_result)
    by (apply HL; exact HxL).
  set (δ1 := extend_env (fun _ => tm_unit public) x t1).
  set (δ2 := extend_env (fun _ => tm_unit public) x t2).
  assert (Henv : env_rel (update empty x T_secret) δ1 δ2).
  { split.
    - intros y. unfold δ1, δ2. unfold extend_env.
      destruct (Nat.eqb y x).
      + split; apply has_type_lc; assumption.
      + split; exact I.
    - intros y T' Hlook. unfold δ1, δ2. unfold extend_env.
      destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
      + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst y.
        unfold update in Hlook. simpl in Hlook.
        rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in Hlook. simpl in Hlook.
        injection Hlook as HT'. subst T'.
        rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. simpl.
        apply R_of_V_value.
        * apply has_type_lc; exact Ht1.
        * apply has_type_lc; exact Ht2.
        * apply V_high; exact Hsi.
      + unfold update in Hlook. simpl in Hlook. rewrite E in Hlook. discriminate Hlook.
  }
  pose proof (fundamental _ _ _ Hty δ1 δ2 Henv) as HR.
  assert (Hprem1 : forall y : atom, In y (fv_ctx C) -> δ1 y = tm_fvar y).
  { intros y Hy. unfold δ1, extend_env.
    assert (Hne : (y =? x) = false) by (apply Nat.eqb_neq; intro Hc; subst y; exact (HxC Hy)).
    rewrite Hne. reflexivity. }
  assert (Hprem2 : forall y : atom, In y (fv_ctx C) -> δ2 y = tm_fvar y).
  { intros y Hy. unfold δ2, extend_env.
    assert (Hne : (y =? x) = false) by (apply Nat.eqb_neq; intro Hc; subst y; exact (HxC Hy)).
    rewrite Hne. reflexivity. }
  assert (Hsub1 : subst_all δ1 (tm_fvar x) = t1).
  { unfold δ1, extend_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. }
  assert (Hsub2 : subst_all δ2 (tm_fvar x) = t2).
  { unfold δ2, extend_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. }
  assert (Hrew1 : subst_all δ1 (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t1).
  { rewrite (subst_all_plug δ1 C (tm_fvar x) Hprem1). rewrite Hsub1. reflexivity. }
  assert (Hrew2 : subst_all δ2 (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t2).
  { rewrite (subst_all_plug δ2 C (tm_fvar x) Hprem2). rewrite Hsub2. reflexivity. }
  rewrite Hrew1, Hrew2 in HR.
  intros v1 v2 Hev1 Hev2.
  apply (adequacy_at T_result v1 v2 Hg (security_of T_result) Htr Hri).
  apply HR; assumption.
Qed.
```

```
Theorem noninterference : forall (C : program_context) (t1 t2 : tm)
    (T_secret T_result : ty),
  has_type empty t1 T_secret ->
  has_type empty t2 T_secret ->
  context_has_type C T_secret T_result ->

  transparent T_result ->
  ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)
      (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) ->
  same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).
```

Let me summarize the work for the user.

Summary of the proof architecture:
1. Logical relation V on values at observer level Low:
   - Universal (True) if the type's indirect_reader is High.
   - Structural otherwise, with each value carrying a security annotation that must flow to its type's annotation (used to show runtime protections are identities in observable cases).
   - Unit: both are units; Sum/Prod: matching constructors with related payloads; Arrow: abs values with security_le to the type, and applications of related arguments yield related results.
2. Term relation R: all completed evaluations produce V-related values.
3. Fundamental theorem by induction on the typing derivation, with environments mapping atoms to terms; locally-nameless substitution lemmas (subst_all, opening).
4. Subtyping lemma: subtype_V (with contravariance on arrow domains).
5. Adequacy: related values at a ground transparent type with Low indirect_reader have equal erasures.
6. The main theorem: derive secret.ind = High and result.ind = Low from the non-flow hypothesis; pick a fresh variable; build an environment mapping it to t1/t2 (V is universal at the secret type so R holds); apply the fundamental theorem through the program context (substitution lemma for plug); apply adequacy.
